### Meson Build Configuration Options (Shell)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/install-meson

Demonstrates how to configure the PostgreSQL build using Meson with various command-line options. These examples show how to specify an installation prefix, enable debug builds, and include OpenSSL support during the Meson setup phase. These options allow customization of the build process.

```shell
# configure with a different installation prefix
meson setup build --prefix=/home/user/pg-install

# configure to generate a debug build
Meson setup build --buildtype=debug

# configure to build with OpenSSL support
Meson setup build -Dssl=openssl
```

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/install-make

This is a condensed sequence of shell commands for building and installing PostgreSQL from source. It includes configuration, compilation, installation, user setup, data directory initialization, server start, and basic database operations.

```shell

make
su
make install
adduser postgres
mkdir -p /usr/local/pgsql/data
chown postgres /usr/local/pgsql/data
su - postgres
/usr/local/pgsql/bin/initdb -D /usr/local/pgsql/data
/usr/local/pgsql/bin/pg_ctl -D /usr/local/pgsql/data -l logfile start
/usr/local/pgsql/bin/createdb test
/usr/local/pgsql/bin/psql test
```

### PostgreSQL Transform Example Setup

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createtransform

Illustrates the necessary steps to set up a transform for the 'hstore' type and 'plpython3u' language in PostgreSQL. This includes creating the data type, extending the procedural language, and defining the required conversion functions.

```sql
CREATE TYPE hstore ...;

CREATE EXTENSION plpython3u;
```

```sql
CREATE FUNCTION hstore_to_plpython(val internal) RETURNS internal
LANGUAGE C STRICT IMMUTABLE
AS ...;

CREATE FUNCTION plpython_to_hstore(val internal) RETURNS hstore
LANGUAGE C STRICT IMMUTABLE
AS ...;
```

```sql
CREATE TRANSFORM FOR hstore LANGUAGE plpython3u (
    FROM SQL WITH FUNCTION hstore_to_plpython(internal),
    TO SQL WITH FUNCTION plpython_to_hstore(internal)
);
```

### Start PostgreSQL Server on OpenBSD

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/server-start

This script snippet shows how to add PostgreSQL server startup to the `/etc/rc.local` file on OpenBSD. It checks for the executability of `pg_ctl` and `postgres`, then starts the server as the `postgres` user using `pg_ctl start`, specifying logging and data directory. This ensures the server starts on boot.

```shell
if [ -x /usr/local/pgsql/bin/pg_ctl -a -x /usr/local/pgsql/bin/postgres ]; then
    su -l postgres -c '/usr/local/pgsql/bin/pg_ctl start -s -l /var/postgresql/log -D /usr/local/pgsql/data'
    echo -n ' postgresql'
fi
```

### C: PostgreSQL Full Procedure with GET DESCRIPTOR Example

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ecpg-sql-get-descriptor

This C code example demonstrates a complete procedure for executing a PostgreSQL query ('SELECT current_database();'), managing a descriptor area, and then using GET DESCRIPTOR to retrieve and print the column count, returned octet length, and column data. It includes connection, cursor management, and descriptor allocation/deallocation.

```c
int
main(void)
{
EXEC SQL BEGIN DECLARE SECTION;
    int  d_count;
    char d_data[1024];
    int  d_returned_octet_length;
EXEC SQL END DECLARE SECTION;

    EXEC SQL CONNECT TO testdb AS con1 USER testuser;
    EXEC SQL SELECT pg_catalog.set_config('search_path', '', false); EXEC SQL COMMIT;
    EXEC SQL ALLOCATE DESCRIPTOR d;

    /* Declare, open a cursor, and assign a descriptor to the cursor  */
    EXEC SQL DECLARE cur CURSOR FOR SELECT current_database();
    EXEC SQL OPEN cur;
    EXEC SQL FETCH NEXT FROM cur INTO SQL DESCRIPTOR d;

    /* Get a number of total columns */
    EXEC SQL GET DESCRIPTOR d :d_count = COUNT;
    printf("d_count                 = %d\n", d_count);

    /* Get length of a returned column */
    EXEC SQL GET DESCRIPTOR d VALUE 1 :d_returned_octet_length = RETURNED_OCTET_LENGTH;
    printf("d_returned_octet_length = %d\n", d_returned_octet_length);

    /* Fetch the returned column as a string */
    EXEC SQL GET DESCRIPTOR d VALUE 1 :d_data = DATA;
    printf("d_data                  = %s\n", d_data);

    /* Closing */
    EXEC SQL CLOSE cur;
    EXEC SQL COMMIT;

    EXEC SQL DEALLOCATE DESCRIPTOR d;
    EXEC SQL DISCONNECT ALL;

    return 0;
}

```

### libpq Example 1: C Program for Database Interaction

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-example

This C program demonstrates basic interaction with a PostgreSQL database using libpq. It connects to the database, sets the search path, starts a transaction, uses a cursor to fetch data from pg_database, and then cleans up. Dependencies include the libpq-fe.h header file.

```c
/*
 * src/test/examples/testlibpq.c
 *
 *
 * testlibpq.c
 *
 *      Test the C version of libpq, the PostgreSQL frontend library.
 */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include "libpq-fe.h"

static void
exit_nicely(PGconn *conn)
{
    PQfinish(conn);
    exit(1);
}

int
main(int argc, char **argv)
{
    const char *conninfo;
    PGconn     *conn;
    PGresult   *res;
    int         nFields;
    int         i, 
                j;

    /*
     * If the user supplies a parameter on the command line, use it as the
     * conninfo string; otherwise default to setting dbname=postgres and using
     * environment variables or defaults for all other connection parameters.
     */
    if (argc > 1)
        conninfo = argv[1];
    else
        conninfo = "dbname = postgres";

    /* Make a connection to the database */
    conn = PQconnectdb(conninfo);

    /* Check to see that the backend connection was successfully made */
    if (PQstatus(conn) != CONNECTION_OK)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "%s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
        exit_nicely(conn);
    }

    /* Set always-secure search path, so malicious users can't take control. */
    res = PQexec(conn,
                 "SELECT pg_catalog.set_config('search_path', '', false)");
    if (PQresultStatus(res) != PGRES_TUPLES_OK)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "SET failed: %s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
        PQclear(res);
        exit_nicely(conn);
    }

    /*
     * Should PQclear PGresult whenever it is no longer needed to avoid memory
     * leaks
     */
    PQclear(res);

    /*
     * Our test case here involves using a cursor, for which we must be inside
     * a transaction block.  We could do the whole thing with a single
     * PQexec() of "select * from pg_database", but that's too trivial to make
     * a good example.
     */

    /* Start a transaction block */
    res = PQexec(conn, "BEGIN");
    if (PQresultStatus(res) != PGRES_COMMAND_OK)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "BEGIN command failed: %s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
        PQclear(res);
        exit_nicely(conn);
    }
    PQclear(res);

    /*
     * Fetch rows from pg_database, the system catalog of databases
     */
    res = PQexec(conn, "DECLARE myportal CURSOR FOR select * from pg_database");
    if (PQresultStatus(res) != PGRES_COMMAND_OK)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "DECLARE CURSOR failed: %s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
        PQclear(res);
        exit_nicely(conn);
    }
    PQclear(res);

    res = PQexec(conn, "FETCH ALL in myportal");
    if (PQresultStatus(res) != PGRES_TUPLES_OK)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "FETCH ALL failed: %s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
        PQclear(res);
        exit_nicely(conn);
    }

    /* first, print out the attribute names */
    nFields = PQnfields(res);
    for (i = 0; i < nFields; i++)
        printf("% -15s", PQfname(res, i));
    printf("\n\n");

    /* next, print out the rows */
    for (i = 0; i < PQntuples(res); i++)
    {
        for (j = 0; j < nFields; j++)
            printf("% -15s", PQgetvalue(res, i, j));
        printf("\n");
    }

    PQclear(res);

    /* close the portal ... we don't bother to check for errors ... */
    res = PQexec(conn, "CLOSE myportal");
    PQclear(res);

    /* end the transaction */
    res = PQexec(conn, "END");
    PQclear(res);

    /* close the connection to the database and cleanup */
    PQfinish(conn);

    return 0;
}

```

### Start an online backup in PostgreSQL

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-admin

The `pg_backup_start` function prepares the server for an online backup, requiring a label for the backup. An optional boolean parameter can force a quick start, causing an I/O spike. This function is restricted to superusers by default. It returns the starting WAL location.

```sql
SELECT pg_backup_start('my_backup_label');
-- Or with fast option:
SELECT pg_backup_start('my_backup_label', true);
```

### Connecting to PostgreSQL and Executing Queries with Parameters (libpq C Example)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-example

This C code demonstrates how to connect to a PostgreSQL database using libpq, execute SQL queries with parameters, and retrieve results. It shows two scenarios: sending text parameters and receiving binary results, and sending binary integer parameters while also receiving binary results. Error handling for connection and query execution is included.

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <libpq-fe.h>
#include <arpa/inet.h>

/* Define field numbers for convenience */
int i_fnum, t_fnum, b_fnum;

static void exit_nicely(PGconn *conn)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "%s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
    PQfinish(conn);
    exit(1);
}

/* Include show_binary_results function here */

int
main(int argc, char **argv)
{
    const char *conninfo;
    PGconn     *conn;
    PGresult   *res;
    const char *paramValues[1];
    int         paramLengths[1];
    int         paramFormats[1];
    uint32_t    binaryIntVal;

    /*
     * If the user supplies a parameter on the command line, use it as the
     * conninfo string; otherwise default to setting dbname=postgres and using
     * environment variables or defaults for all other connection parameters.
     */
    if (argc > 1)
        conninfo = argv[1];
    else
        conninfo = "dbname = postgres";

    /* Make a connection to the database */
    conn = PQconnectdb(conninfo);

    /* Check to see that the backend connection was successfully made */
    if (PQstatus(conn) != CONNECTION_OK)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "%s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
        exit_nicely(conn);
    }

    /* Set always-secure search path, so malicious users can't take control. */
    res = PQexec(conn, "SET search_path = testlibpq3");
    if (PQresultStatus(res) != PGRES_COMMAND_OK)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "SET failed: %s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
        PQclear(res);
        exit_nicely(conn);
    }
    PQclear(res);

    /*
     * The point of this program is to illustrate use of PQexecParams() with
     * out-of-line parameters, as well as binary transmission of data.
     *
     * This first example transmits the parameters as text, but receives the
     * results in binary format.  By using out-of-line parameters we can avoid
     * a lot of tedious mucking about with quoting and escaping, even though
     * the data is text.  Notice how we don't have to do anything special with
     * the quote mark in the parameter value.
     */

    /* Here is our out-of-line parameter value */
    paramValues[0] = "joe's place";

    res = PQexecParams(conn,
                       "SELECT * FROM test1 WHERE t = $1",
                       1,       /* one param */
                       NULL,    /* let the backend deduce param type */
                       paramValues,
                       NULL,    /* don't need param lengths since text */
                       NULL,    /* default to all text params */
                       1);      /* ask for binary results */

    if (PQresultStatus(res) != PGRES_TUPLES_OK)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "SELECT failed: %s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
        PQclear(res);
        exit_nicely(conn);
    }

    /* Assuming show_binary_results is defined elsewhere or above */
    /* show_binary_results(res); */

    PQclear(res);

    /*
     * In this second example we transmit an integer parameter in binary form,
     * and again retrieve the results in binary form.
     *
     * Although we tell PQexecParams we are letting the backend deduce
     * parameter type, we really force the decision by casting the parameter
     * symbol in the query text.  This is a good safety measure when sending
     * binary parameters.
     */

    /* Convert integer value "2" to network byte order */
    binaryIntVal = htonl((uint32_t) 2);

    /* Set up parameter arrays for PQexecParams */
    paramValues[0] = (char *) &binaryIntVal;
    paramLengths[0] = sizeof(binaryIntVal);
    paramFormats[0] = 1;        /* binary */

    res = PQexecParams(conn,
                       "SELECT * FROM test1 WHERE i = $1::int4",
                       1,       /* one param */
                       NULL,    /* let the backend deduce param type */
                       paramValues,
                       paramLengths,
                       paramFormats,
                       1);      /* ask for binary results */

    if (PQresultStatus(res) != PGRES_TUPLES_OK)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "SELECT failed: %s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
        PQclear(res);
        exit_nicely(conn);
    }

    /* Assuming show_binary_results is defined elsewhere or above */
    /* show_binary_results(res); */

    PQclear(res);

    /* close the connection to the database and cleanup */
    PQfinish(conn);

    return 0;
}

```

### PostgreSQL CONNECT Command Examples

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ecpg-sql-connect

Demonstrates various ways to use the CONNECT command in SQL, covering different parameter formats for connection targets, users, and connection names.

```sql
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO "connectdb" AS main;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO "connectdb" AS second;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO "unix:postgresql://200.46.204.71/connectdb" AS main USER connectuser;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO "unix:postgresql://localhost/connectdb" AS main USER connectuser;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO 'connectdb' AS main;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO 'unix:postgresql://localhost/connectdb' AS main USER :user;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO :db AS :id;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO :db USER connectuser USING :pw;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO @localhost AS main USER connectdb;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO REGRESSDB1 as main;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO AS main USER connectdb;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO connectdb AS :id;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO connectdb AS main USER connectuser/connectdb;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO connectdb AS main;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO connectdb@localhost AS main;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO tcp:postgresql://localhost/ USER connectdb;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO tcp:postgresql://localhost/connectdb USER connectuser IDENTIFIED BY connectpw;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO tcp:postgresql://localhost:20/connectdb USER connectuser IDENTIFIED BY connectpw;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO unix:postgresql://localhost/ AS main USER connectdb;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO unix:postgresql://localhost/connectdb AS main USER connectuser;
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO unix:postgresql://localhost/connectdb USER connectuser IDENTIFIED BY "connectpw";
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO unix:postgresql://localhost/connectdb USER connectuser USING "connectpw";
EXEC SQL CONNECT TO unix:postgresql://localhost/connectdb?connect_timeout=14 USER connectuser;
```

### Start PostgreSQL Server with pg_ctl (as PostgreSQL User)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/server-start

This command demonstrates starting the PostgreSQL server using `pg_ctl` with the correct user privileges. It uses `su` to execute `pg_ctl start` as the `postgres` user, ensuring the server runs with appropriate permissions. The `-D` and `-l` options specify the data directory and log file respectively.

```shell
su postgres -c 'pg_ctl start -D /usr/local/pgsql/data -l serverlog'
```

### PostgreSQL format() basic usage examples

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-string

Demonstrates basic usage of the format() function with string and literal formatting specifiers. Shows how to insert strings and handle special characters.

```sql
SELECT format('Hello %s', 'World');
-- Result: Hello World

SELECT format('Testing %s, %s, %s, %%', 'one', 'two', 'three');
-- Result: Testing one, two, three, %

SELECT format('INSERT INTO %I VALUES(%L)', 'Foo bar', E'O\'Reilly');
-- Result: INSERT INTO "Foo bar" VALUES('O\'Reilly')

SELECT format('INSERT INTO %I VALUES(%L)', 'locations', 'C:\Program Files');
-- Result: INSERT INTO locations VALUES('C:\Program Files')
```

### CREATE COLLATION Examples

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createcollation

Examples demonstrating how to create collations in PostgreSQL using different methods.

```APIDOC
## CREATE COLLATION

### Description
This section provides examples for creating collations in PostgreSQL.

### Examples

1.  **Create collation from operating system locale:**
    ```sql
    CREATE COLLATION french (locale = 'fr_FR.utf8');
    ```

2.  **Create collation using ICU provider with German phone book sort order:**
    ```sql
    CREATE COLLATION german_phonebook (provider = icu, locale = 'de-u-co-phonebk');
    ```

3.  **Create collation using ICU provider with custom rules:**
    ```sql
    CREATE COLLATION custom (provider = icu, locale = 'und', rules = '&V << w <<< W');
    ```
    *See Section 23.2.3.4 for further details and examples on the rules syntax.*

4.  **Create collation from an existing collation:**
    ```sql
    CREATE COLLATION german FROM "de_DE";
    ```
    *This can be convenient to be able to use operating-system-independent collation names in applications.*

### Compatibility

The SQL standard includes a `CREATE COLLATION` statement, but it is limited to copying an existing collation. The syntax to create a new collation is a PostgreSQL extension.

### See Also

*   ALTER COLLATION
*   DROP COLLATION
```

### PostgreSQL POSIX Regular Expression Examples

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-matching

Illustrates various POSIX regular expression patterns and their matches in PostgreSQL. This includes matching any character (.), repeating patterns (*), grouping with OR (|), and anchoring to the start of the string (^).

```SQL
SELECT 'abcd' ~ 'bc'; -- true
SELECT 'abcd' ~ 'a.c'; -- true
SELECT 'abcd' ~ 'a.*d'; -- true
SELECT 'abcd' ~ '(b|x)'; -- true
SELECT 'abcd' ~ '^a'; -- true
SELECT 'abcd' ~ '^(b|c)'; -- false
```

### PostgreSQL Transaction and Statement Time Functions

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-datetime

Provides functions to get the transaction start time, statement start time, or the actual current time. `transaction_timestamp()` is equivalent to `CURRENT_TIMESTAMP`, `statement_timestamp()` returns the start of the current statement, and `clock_timestamp()` returns the actual current time.

```sql
SELECT transaction_timestamp();
SELECT statement_timestamp();
SELECT clock_timestamp();
SELECT timeofday();
SELECT now();
```

### Connect to PostgreSQL and Listen for Notifications (C)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-example

Establishes a connection to a PostgreSQL database using connection information, sets a secure search path, and then listens for notifications on a specified channel ('TBL2'). The code waits for and processes up to four notifications asynchronously using `select()` and `PQconsumeInput()` before disconnecting.

```c
/*
 * environment variables or defaults for all other connection parameters.
 */
if (argc > 1)
    conninfo = argv[1];
else
    conninfo = "dbname = postgres";

/* Make a connection to the database */
conn = PQconnectdb(conninfo);

/* Check to see that the backend connection was successfully made */
if (PQstatus(conn) != CONNECTION_OK)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "%s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
    exit_nicely(conn);
}

/* Set always-secure search path, so malicious users can't take control. */
res = PQexec(
             conn,
             "SELECT pg_catalog.set_config('search_path', '', false)"
           );
if (PQresultStatus(res) != PGRES_TUPLES_OK)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "SET failed: %s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
    PQclear(res);
    exit_nicely(conn);
}

/*
 * Should PQclear PGresult whenever it is no longer needed to avoid memory
 * leaks
 */
PQclear(res);

/*
 * Issue LISTEN command to enable notifications from the rule's NOTIFY.
 */
res = PQexec(conn, "LISTEN TBL2");
if (PQresultStatus(res) != PGRES_COMMAND_OK)
{
    fprintf(stderr, "LISTEN command failed: %s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
    PQclear(res);
    exit_nicely(conn);
}
PQclear(res);

/* Quit after four notifies are received. */
nnotifies = 0;
while (nnotifies < 4)
{
    /*
     * Sleep until something happens on the connection.  We use select(2)
     * to wait for input, but you could also use poll() or similar
     * facilities.
     */
    int         sock;
    fd_set      input_mask;

    sock = PQsocket(conn);

    if (sock < 0)
        break;              /* shouldn't happen */

    FD_ZERO(&input_mask);
    FD_SET(sock, &input_mask);

    if (select(sock + 1, &input_mask, NULL, NULL, NULL) < 0)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "select() failed: %s\n", strerror(errno));
        exit_nicely(conn);
    }

    /* Now check for input */
    PQconsumeInput(conn);
    while ((notify = PQnotifies(conn)) != NULL)
    {
        fprintf(stderr,
                "ASYNC NOTIFY of '%s' received from backend PID %d\n",
                notify->relname, notify->be_pid);
        PQfreemem(notify);
        nnotifies++;
        PQconsumeInput(conn);
    }
}

fprintf(stderr, "Done.\n");

/* close the connection to the database and cleanup */
PQfinish(conn);

return 0;
}

```

### PostgreSQL SELinux: Get and Set Security Context

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sepgsql

Demonstrates retrieving the current SELinux security context of a PostgreSQL client and attempting to transition to a new context. The `sepgsql_getcon()` function retrieves the current label, while `sepgsql_setcon()` attempts to change it. The example highlights successful transitions to a smaller privilege set and denied transitions to a larger one, illustrating policy enforcement.

```sql
regression=# select sepgsql_getcon();
                    sepgsql_getcon
-------------------------------------------------------
 unconfined_u:unconfined_r:unconfined_t:s0-s0:c0.c1023
(1 row)

regression=# SELECT sepgsql_setcon('unconfined_u:unconfined_r:unconfined_t:s0-s0:c1.c4');
 sepgsql_setcon
----------------
 t
(1 row)

regression=# SELECT sepgsql_setcon('unconfined_u:unconfined_r:unconfined_t:s0-s0:c1.c1023');
ERROR:  SELinux: security policy violation

```

### Troubleshooting PostgreSQL Build Errors on Solaris

Undefined                       first referenced
 symbol                             in file
AbortTransaction                    utils/probes.o

ld: fatal: Symbol referencing errors. No output written to postgres
collect2: ld returned 1 exit status

### Check for Overlapping Time Periods using OVERLAPS

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-datetime

The `OVERLAPS` operator determines if two time periods overlap. Periods can be defined by start and end points, or start point and length. The operator returns `true` if they overlap and `false` otherwise. It treats periods as half-open intervals `[start, end)`. The examples illustrate overlapping date ranges and edge cases.

```sql
SELECT (DATE '2001-02-16', DATE '2001-12-21') OVERLAPS
       (DATE '2001-10-30', DATE '2002-10-30');
-- Result: true

SELECT (DATE '2001-02-16', INTERVAL '100 days') OVERLAPS
       (DATE '2001-10-30', DATE '2002-10-30');
-- Result: false

SELECT (DATE '2001-10-29', DATE '2001-10-30') OVERLAPS
       (DATE '2001-10-30', DATE '2001-10-31');
-- Result: false

SELECT (DATE '2001-10-30', DATE '2001-10-30') OVERLAPS
       (DATE '2001-10-30', DATE '2001-10-31');
-- Result: true
```

### Obtain Execution Location and Call Stack in PL/pgSQL using GET DIAGNOSTICS

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/plpgsql-control-structures

This example shows how to use the GET DIAGNOSTICS command with the PG_CONTEXT item in PL/pgSQL to retrieve the current execution location and call stack. The PG_CONTEXT value is then raised as a notice, providing valuable debugging information about function calls. This is distinct from GET STACKED DIAGNOSTICS, which is used for error context.

```plpgsql
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION outer_func() RETURNS integer AS $$
BEGIN
  RETURN inner_func();
END;
$$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION inner_func() RETURNS integer AS $$
DECLARE
  stack text;
BEGIN
  GET DIAGNOSTICS stack = PG_CONTEXT;
  RAISE NOTICE E'--- Call Stack ---\n%', stack;
  RETURN 1;
END;
$$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;

SELECT outer_func();
```

### Get Backend Activity Information

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/monitoring-stats

Retrieves information about active backend processes, including their PIDs, current queries, and start times. This function is part of a set of per-backend statistics access functions.

```APIDOC
## GET PostgreSQL Backend Activity Information

### Description
Provides access to per-backend statistics. The `pg_stat_get_backend_idset()` function returns a set of active backend ID numbers, which can then be used with other `pg_stat_get_backend_*` functions to retrieve specific details about each backend.

### Method
`SELECT` (This is a PostgreSQL function call)

### Endpoint
N/A (Function call within PostgreSQL)

### Parameters
#### Path Parameters
None

#### Query Parameters
None

#### Request Body
None

### Request Example
```sql
-- Show PIDs and current queries of all backends
SELECT pg_stat_get_backend_pid(backendid) AS pid,
       pg_stat_get_backend_activity(backendid) AS query
FROM pg_stat_get_backend_idset() AS backendid;

-- Get client IP address for a specific backend ID
SELECT pg_stat_get_backend_client_addr(123) AS client_ip;
```

### Response
#### Success Response
Returns records with the requested backend information. The type depends on the specific `pg_stat_get_backend_*` function used.

*   `pg_stat_get_backend_activity(integer)` → `text`: Text of the backend's most recent query.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_activity_start(integer)` → `timestamp with time zone`: Time when the backend's most recent query was started.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_client_addr(integer)` → `inet`: IP address of the client connected to this backend.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_client_port(integer)` → `integer`: TCP port number that the client is using for communication.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_dbid(integer)` → `oid`: OID of the database this backend is connected to.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_idset()` → `setof integer`: Set of currently active backend ID numbers.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_pid(integer)` → `integer`: Process ID of this backend.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_start(integer)` → `timestamp with time zone`: Time when this process was started.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_subxact(integer)` → `record`: Record of information about the subtransactions of the backend.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_userid(integer)` → `oid`: OID of the user logged into this backend.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_wait_event(integer)` → `text`: Wait event name if this backend is currently waiting, otherwise NULL.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_wait_event_type(integer)` → `text`: Wait event type name if this backend is currently waiting, otherwise NULL.
*   `pg_stat_get_backend_xact_start(integer)` → `timestamp with time zone`: Time when the backend's current transaction was started.

#### Response Example
(Output format varies based on the specific function called. Example for `pg_stat_get_backend_idset()` and `pg_stat_get_backend_activity()`):
```json
[{"pid": 12345, "query": "SELECT * FROM my_table;"},
 {"pid": 67890, "query": "UPDATE another_table SET col = 1;"}]
```
```

### PostgreSQL LIKE Operator Syntax and Examples

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-matching

Demonstrates the basic syntax and usage of the LIKE operator in PostgreSQL for pattern matching. It shows how wildcard characters '%' and '_' function, and provides examples of true and false matches.

```sql
_string_ LIKE _pattern_ [ESCAPE _escape-character_]
_string_ NOT LIKE _pattern_ [ESCAPE _escape-character_]

-- Examples:
'abc' LIKE 'abc'    -- true
'abc' LIKE 'a%'     -- true
'abc' LIKE '_b_'    -- true
'abc' LIKE 'c'      -- false

```

### PostgreSQL MOVE Command Syntax and Example

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-move

Demonstrates the syntax for the PostgreSQL MOVE command and provides a practical example of using MOVE to reposition a cursor within a transaction before fetching a row. The example includes the DECLARE CURSOR, MOVE FORWARD, FETCH, and CLOSE commands.

```sql
BEGIN WORK;
DECLARE liahona CURSOR FOR SELECT * FROM films;

-- Skip the first 5 rows:
MOVE FORWARD 5 IN liahona;
MOVE 5

-- Fetch the 6th row from the cursor liahona:
FETCH 1 FROM liahona;
-- Expected output:
--  code  | title  | did | date_prod  |  kind  |  len
-- -------+--------+-----+------------+--------+-------
--  P_303 | 48 Hrs | 103 | 1982-10-22 | Action | 01:37
-- (1 row)

-- Close the cursor liahona and end the transaction:
CLOSE liahona;
COMMIT WORK;
```

### Manual Procedural Language Installation Steps

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/xplang-install

A five-step process for manually installing a procedural language, intended for languages not packaged as extensions. This requires superuser privileges.

```APIDOC
## Manual Procedural Language Installation

### Description
Provides a detailed, five-step guide for manually installing a procedural language. This process involves compiling and declaring handler, inline, and validator functions.

### Method
SQL

### Endpoint
N/A (SQL Commands)

### Parameters
No direct parameters, but requires specific SQL commands to be executed in sequence.

### Steps

1.  **Compile and Install Shared Object**: Compile the language handler's shared object and install it into the appropriate library directory. Ensure any external library dependencies are also installed.

2.  **Declare Handler Function**: Register the language handler function using `CREATE FUNCTION`.
    ```sql
    CREATE FUNCTION _handler_function_name_()
        RETURNS language_handler
        AS '_path-to-shared-object_'
        LANGUAGE C;
    ```

3.  **Declare Inline Handler Function (Optional)**: If the language supports anonymous code blocks (`DO` commands), declare the inline handler function.
    ```sql
    CREATE FUNCTION _inline_function_name_(internal)
        RETURNS void
        AS '_path-to-shared-object_'
        LANGUAGE C;
    ```

4.  **Declare Validator Function (Optional)**: If a validator function exists to check function definitions, declare it.
    ```sql
    CREATE FUNCTION _validator_function_name_(oid)
        RETURNS void
        AS '_path-to-shared-object_'
        LANGUAGE C STRICT;
    ```

5.  **Declare the Language**: Finally, declare the procedural language using `CREATE LANGUAGE`.
    ```sql
    CREATE [TRUSTED] LANGUAGE _language_name_
        HANDLER _handler_function_name_
        [INLINE _inline_function_name_]
        [VALIDATOR _validator_function_name_];
    ```
    The `TRUSTED` keyword indicates if the language is safe for non-superusers.

### Request Example (PL/Perl Manual Installation)
```sql
-- Declare the call handler function
CREATE FUNCTION plperl_call_handler() RETURNS language_handler AS
    '$libdir/plperl' LANGUAGE C;

-- Declare the inline handler function
CREATE FUNCTION plperl_inline_handler(internal) RETURNS void AS
    '$libdir/plperl' LANGUAGE C STRICT;

-- Declare the validator function
CREATE FUNCTION plperl_validator(oid) RETURNS void AS
    '$libdir/plperl' LANGUAGE C STRICT;

-- Declare the PL/Perl language as trusted
CREATE TRUSTED LANGUAGE plperl
    HANDLER plperl_call_handler
    INLINE plperl_inline_handler
    VALIDATOR plperl_validator;
```

### Response
#### Success Response (0)
- SQL commands executed successfully, registering the procedural language.

#### Response Example
```
(No output, command successful)
```
```

### PostgreSQL: Role Membership Example with Inheritance and SET ROLE

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/role-membership

An example illustrating the creation of roles and the granting of membership with specific options like INHERIT and SET, demonstrating how privileges are managed.

```sql
CREATE ROLE joe LOGIN;

CREATE ROLE island;

GRANT wheel TO admin WITH INHERIT FALSE;

```

### Example: Creating Rules in PostgreSQL

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createrule

Demonstrates the syntax for creating rules in PostgreSQL. This example shows how to create two rules that reference each other, leading to a recursive expansion error. It also includes examples of creating a rule for UPDATE with a NOTIFY command.

```sql
CREATE RULE "_RETURN" AS
    ON SELECT TO t1
    DO INSTEAD
        SELECT * FROM t2;

CREATE RULE "_RETURN" AS
    ON SELECT TO t2
    DO INSTEAD
        SELECT * FROM t1;

SELECT * FROM t1;

-- Example of a NOTIFY rule
CREATE RULE notify_me AS ON UPDATE TO mytable DO ALSO NOTIFY mytable;

UPDATE mytable SET name = 'foo' WHERE id = 42;
```

### PostgreSQL crosstab Example: Complete Implementation

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/tablefunc

Provides a complete, runnable example demonstrating the crosstab function. This includes creating a sample table, inserting data, and then using crosstab with a specific query and output column definition to pivot the data.

```sql
CREATE TABLE ct(id SERIAL, rowid TEXT, attribute TEXT, value TEXT);
INSERT INTO ct(rowid, attribute, value) VALUES('test1','att1','val1');
INSERT INTO ct(rowid, attribute, value) VALUES('test1','att2','val2');
INSERT INTO ct(rowid, attribute, value) VALUES('test1','att3','val3');
INSERT INTO ct(rowid, attribute, value) VALUES('test1','att4','val4');
INSERT INTO ct(rowid, attribute, value) VALUES('test2','att1','val5');
INSERT INTO ct(rowid, attribute, value) VALUES('test2','att2','val6');
INSERT INTO ct(rowid, attribute, value) VALUES('test2','att3','val7');
INSERT INTO ct(rowid, attribute, value) VALUES('test2','att4','val8');

SELECT *
FROM crosstab(
  'select rowid, attribute, value
   from ct
   where attribute = ''att2'' or attribute = ''att3''
   order by 1,2')
AS ct(row_name text, category_1 text, category_2 text, category_3 text);

```

### PostgreSQL regexp_replace

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-matching

Details the `regexp_replace` function, which substitutes new text for substrings matching POSIX regular expression patterns. It covers syntax, parameters like `string`, `pattern`, `replacement`, `start`, `N`, and `flags`, and provides examples.

```APIDOC
## PostgreSQL regexp_replace

### Description
The `regexp_replace` function substitutes new text for substrings that match POSIX regular expression patterns. It allows for specifying the string, pattern, replacement text, optional start position, occurrence number, and flags to modify behavior.

### Method
N/A (SQL Function)

### Endpoint
N/A (SQL Function)

### Parameters
#### Path Parameters
None

#### Query Parameters
None

#### Request Body
None

### Request Example
```sql
-- Basic replacement
SELECT regexp_replace('foobarbaz', 'b..', 'X');

-- Global replacement
SELECT regexp_replace('foobarbaz', 'b..', 'X', 'g');

-- Replacement using backreferences
SELECT regexp_replace('foobarbaz', 'b(..)', 'X\1Y', 'g');

-- Replacement with flags and specific occurrence
SELECT regexp_replace(string=>'A PostgreSQL function', pattern=>'a|e|i|o|u', replacement=>'X', start=>1, "N"=>3, flags=>'i');
```

### Response
#### Success Response (SQL Result)
- Returns the modified string with replacements made according to the pattern and flags.
- Returns the original string if no match is found.

#### Response Example
```sql
-- Result of 'regexp_replace('foobarbaz', 'b..', 'X')'
-- regexp_replace
-- ------------- 
-- fooXbaz

-- Result of 'regexp_replace('foobarbaz', 'b..', 'X', 'g')'
-- regexp_replace
-- ------------- 
-- fooXX

-- Result of 'regexp_replace('foobarbaz', 'b(..)', 'X\1Y', 'g')'
-- regexp_replace
-- ------------- 
-- fooXarYXazY

-- Result of 'regexp_replace(string=>'A PostgreSQL function', pattern=>'a|e|i|o|u', replacement=>'X', start=>1, "N"=>3, flags=>'i')'
-- regexp_replace
-- ------------------------
-- A PostgrXSQL function
```
```

### Request LWLocks at Startup (C)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/xfunc-c

Add-ins can reserve LWLocks during server startup similarly to shared memory. This involves preloading a shared library and registering a `shmem_request_hook`. The hook calls `RequestNamedLWLockTranche` to reserve an array of LWLocks under a specified name. Pointers to these LWLocks can then be obtained using `GetNamedLWLockTranche`.

```c
void RequestNamedLWLockTranche(const char *tranche_name, int num_lwlocks);

LWLockPadded *GetNamedLWLockTranche(const char *tranche_name);
```

### PostgreSQL format() width and flag examples

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-string

Illustrates how to use width specifiers and the left-alignment flag (-) in the format() function for string padding.

```sql
SELECT format('|%10s|', 'foo');
-- Result: |       foo|

SELECT format('|%-10s|', 'foo');
-- Result: |foo       |

SELECT format('|%*s|', 10, 'foo');
-- Result: |       foo|

SELECT format('|%*s|', -10, 'foo');
-- Result: |foo       |

SELECT format('|%-*s|', 10, 'foo');
-- Result: |foo       |

SELECT format('|%-*s|', -10, 'foo');
-- Result: |foo       |
```

### libpq Example 2: C Program for Asynchronous Notifications

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-example

This C program demonstrates the asynchronous notification interface of libpq. It connects to a PostgreSQL database and listens for notifications. The program exits after receiving a specified number of notifications, which can be triggered by other clients using the NOTIFY command or by specific database events as defined in a SQL script.

```c
/*
 * src/test/examples/testlibpq2.c
 *
 *
 * testlibpq2.c
 *      Test of the asynchronous notification interface
 *
 * Start this program, then from psql in another window do
 *   NOTIFY TBL2;
 * Repeat four times to get this program to exit.
 *
 * Or, if you want to get fancy, try this:
 * populate a database with the following commands
 * (provided in src/test/examples/testlibpq2.sql):
 *
 *   CREATE SCHEMA TESTLIBPQ2;
 *   SET search_path = TESTLIBPQ2;
 *   CREATE TABLE TBL1 (i int4);
 *   CREATE TABLE TBL2 (i int4);
 *   CREATE RULE r1 AS ON INSERT TO TBL1 DO
 *     (INSERT INTO TBL2 VALUES (new.i); NOTIFY TBL2);
 *
 * Start this program, then from psql do this four times:
 *
 *   INSERT INTO TESTLIBPQ2.TBL1 VALUES (10);
 */

#ifdef WIN32
#include <windows.h>
#endif
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <errno.h>
#include <sys/select.h>
#include <sys/time.h> 
#include <sys/types.h> 

#include "libpq-fe.h"

static void
exit_nicely(PGconn *conn)
{
    PQfinish(conn);
    exit(1);
}

int
main(int argc, char **argv)
{
    const char *conninfo;
    PGconn     *conn;
    PGresult   *res;
    PGnotify   *notify;
    int         nnotifies;

    /*
     * If the user supplies a parameter on the command line, use it as the
     * conninfo string; otherwise default to setting dbname=postgres and using

```

### Configure PostgreSQL Server with systemd

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/server-start

This is a systemd service unit file for managing the PostgreSQL server. It defines the service description, dependencies, execution commands for starting and reloading, and kill behavior. It specifies the PostgreSQL user and data directory, and uses `Type=notify` which requires the server binary to be built with systemd support.

```ini
[Unit]
Description=PostgreSQL database server
Documentation=man:postgres(1)
After=network-online.target
Wants=network-online.target

[Service]
Type=notify
User=postgres
ExecStart=/usr/local/pgsql/bin/postgres -D /usr/local/pgsql/data
ExecReload=/bin/kill -HUP $MAINPID
KillMode=mixed
KillSignal=SIGINT
TimeoutSec=infinity

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
```

Creates a publication named 'mypub' on the publisher database, specifying the tables ('users', 'departments') to be included in the replication. This defines what data will be replicated.

```sql
CREATE PUBLICATION mypub FOR TABLE users, departments;
```

### Read Specific Portion of PostgreSQL Large Object using libpq (C)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/lo-examplesect

This function reads a specified portion (defined by start offset and length) of a PostgreSQL large object and prints it to standard error. It opens the large object, seeks to the desired starting position, reads the data into a buffer, and then closes the object. Memory allocated for the buffer is freed before returning. Error handling for opening and reading the large object is included.

```c
static void
pickout(PGconn *conn, Oid lobjId, int start, int len)
{
    int         lobj_fd;
    char       *buf;
    int         nbytes;
    int         nread;

    lobj_fd = lo_open(conn, lobjId, INV_READ);
    if (lobj_fd < 0)
        fprintf(stderr, "cannot open large object %u", lobjId);

    lo_lseek(conn, lobj_fd, start, SEEK_SET);
    buf = malloc(len + 1);

    nread = 0;
    while (len - nread > 0)
    {
        nbytes = lo_read(conn, lobj_fd, buf, len - nread);
        buf[nbytes] = '\0';
        fprintf(stderr, ">>> %s", buf);
        nread += nbytes;
        if (nbytes <= 0)
            break;              /* no more data? */
    }
    free(buf);
    fprintf(stderr, "\n");
    lo_close(conn, lobj_fd);
}
```

### Retrieving Array Dimensions in PostgreSQL

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/arrays

Shows how to use PostgreSQL functions to get information about array dimensions. It includes examples for `array_dims` (textual representation), `array_upper` and `array_lower` (bounds of a dimension), and `array_length` (size of a dimension).

```SQL
SELECT array_dims(schedule) FROM sal_emp WHERE name = 'Carol';

-- Result:
--  array_dims
------------
-- [1:2][1:2]
--(1 row)

```

```SQL
SELECT array_upper(schedule, 1) FROM sal_emp WHERE name = 'Carol';

-- Result:
--  array_upper
-------------
--           2
--(1 row)

```

```SQL
SELECT array_length(schedule, 1) FROM sal_emp WHERE name = 'Carol';

-- Result:
--  array_length

### PostgreSQL EXPLAIN ANALYZE with BUFFERS OFF

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/using-explain

Demonstrates using EXPLAIN ANALYZE with BUFFERS OFF to analyze a query without detailed I/O information. This example uses a query on the 'tenk1' table with multiple conditions and shows a Bitmap Heap Scan, BitmapAnd, and two Bitmap Index Scans.

```sql

                                                           QUERY PLAN
-------------------------------------------------------------------​--------------------------------------------------------------
 Bitmap Heap Scan on tenk1  (cost=25.07..60.11 rows=10 width=244) (actual time=0.105..0.114 rows=10.00 loops=1)
   Recheck Cond: ((unique1 < 100) AND (unique2 > 9000))
   Heap Blocks: exact=10
   ->  BitmapAnd  (cost=25.07..25.07 rows=10 width=0) (actual time=0.100..0.101 rows=0.00 loops=1)
         ->  Bitmap Index Scan on tenk1_unique1  (cost=0.00..5.04 rows=100 width=0) (actual time=0.027..0.027 rows=100.00 loops=1)
               Index Cond: (unique1 < 100)
               Index Searches: 1
         ->  Bitmap Index Scan on tenk1_unique2  (cost=0.00..19.78 rows=999 width=0) (actual time=0.070..0.070 rows=999.00 loops=1)
               Index Cond: (unique2 > 9000)
               Index Searches: 1
 Planning Time: 0.162 ms
 Execution Time: 0.143 ms
```

### Get ACL for Database Object (SQL)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-info

Retrieves the Access Control List (ACL) for a specified database object using its catalog OID, object OID, and sub-object ID. This example demonstrates how to use pg_get_acl in conjunction with pg_identify_object to list object privileges.

```sql
SELECT
    (pg_identify_object(s.classid,s.objid,s.objsubid)).*,
    pg_catalog.pg_get_acl(s.classid,s.objid,s.objsubid) AS acl
FROM pg_catalog.pg_shdepend AS s
JOIN pg_catalog.pg_database AS d
    ON d.datname = current_database() AND
       d.oid = s.dbid
JOIN pg_catalog.pg_authid AS a
    ON a.oid = s.refobjid AND
       s.refclassid = 'pg_authid'::regclass
WHERE s.deptype = 'a';
```

### Start Streaming Changes

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/app-pgrecvlogical

Starts streaming changes from a logical replication slot. Data is written to a specified file or stdout.

```APIDOC
## POST /websites/postgresql_current/replication/stream

### Description
Begins streaming changes from a logical replication slot. The stream format is determined by the output plugin. Requires `--slot`, `--dbname`, and `--file`.

### Method
POST

### Endpoint
`/websites/postgresql_current/replication/stream`

### Parameters
#### Query Parameters
- **--start** (boolean) - Required - Flag to indicate starting stream.
- **--slot** (string) - Required - The name of the logical replication slot to use.
- **--dbname** (string) - Required - The database to connect to (must be the same as used for slot creation).
- **-f, --file** (string) - Required - The file to write decoded transaction data to (use `-` for stdout).
- **--endpos** (string) - Optional - The LSN at which to stop replication.
- **--fsync-interval** (integer) - Optional - Interval in seconds for issuing `fsync()` calls to the output file. `0` disables fsyncs.
- **-I, --startpos** (string) - Optional - The LSN from which to start replication.
- **-n, --no-loop** (boolean) - Optional - Do not retry in a loop if the connection is lost; just exit.
- **-o, --option** (string) - Optional - Pass an option to the output plugin (format: `name` or `name=value`).
- **-s, --status-interval** (integer) - Optional - Interval in seconds for sending status updates to the server.
- **-v, --verbose** (boolean) - Optional - Enables verbose mode.
- **-d, --dbname** (string) - Required - The database to connect to.
- **-h, --host** (string) - Optional - The hostname or IP address of the server.

### Request Example
```bash
pg_recvlogical --start --slot my_slot --dbname my_db --file - --startpos 0/1340B78 --endpos 0/15C5178
```

### Response
#### Success Response (200)
- **status** (string) - Indicates the streaming status.
- **message** (string) - Details about the streaming process or completion.

#### Response Example
```json
{
  "status": "streaming",
  "message": "Streaming changes from slot 'my_slot'. Writing to stdout."
}
```
```

### Full Text Search Indexing and Querying with Soundex

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/fuzzystrmatch

This example demonstrates setting up a GIN index using the custom Full Text Search functions (soundex_tsvector and soundex_tsquery) for phonetic matching. It includes table creation, index definition, data insertion, and various query examples to showcase the advanced search capabilities.

```SQL
CREATE TABLE s (nm text);
CREATE INDEX ix_s_txt ON s USING gin (soundex_tsvector(nm)) WITH (fastupdate = off);

INSERT INTO s (nm) VALUES
  ('John Doe'),
  ('Jane Roe'),
  ('Public John Q.'),
  ('George Best'),
  ('John Yamson');

SELECT * FROM s WHERE soundex_tsvector(nm) @@ soundex_tsquery('john');
SELECT * FROM s WHERE soundex_tsvector(nm) @@ soundex_tsquery('jane doe');
SELECT * FROM s WHERE soundex_tsvector(nm) @@ soundex_tsquery('john public');
SELECT * FROM s WHERE soundex_tsvector(nm) @@ soundex_tsquery('besst, giorgio');
SELECT * FROM s WHERE soundex_tsvector(nm) @@ soundex_tsquery('Jameson John');
```

### PostgreSQL LISTEN and UNLISTEN Example

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-unlisten

Demonstrates how to use LISTEN to register for notifications on a channel ('virtual') and then how UNLISTEN is used to remove that registration. The example shows that after UNLISTEN, subsequent NOTIFY messages on that channel are not received.

```sql
LISTEN virtual;
NOTIFY virtual;
-- Asynchronous notification "virtual" received from server process with PID 8448.

UNLISTEN virtual;
NOTIFY virtual;
-- no NOTIFY event is received
```

### C Example: Managing libpq Connection and Result Instance Data with Events

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-events

This C code snippet demonstrates how to implement and use a custom event procedure with libpq. It shows how to register an event handler for connection and result events, associate custom data structures (instance data) with libpq connection and result objects, and manage the lifecycle of this data through various libpq operations such as connecting, executing queries, copying results, and disconnecting. The example utilizes PGEVT_REGISTER, PGEVT_CONNDESTROY, PGEVT_RESULTCREATE, PGEVT_RESULTCOPY, and PGEVT_RESULTDESTROY events.

```c
#include <libpq-events.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>

/* The instanceData */
typedef struct
{
    int n;
    char *str;
} mydata;

/* Forward declarations for helper functions (assuming these are defined elsewhere) */
mydata *get_mydata(PGconn *conn);
mydata *dup_mydata(mydata *src);
void free_mydata(mydata *data);

/* PGEventProc prototype */
static int myEventProc(PGEventId evtId, void *evtInfo, void *passThrough);

int
main(void)
{
    mydata *data;
    PGresult *res;
    PGconn *conn = 
        PQconnectdb("dbname=postgres options=-csearch_path=");

    if (PQstatus(conn) != CONNECTION_OK)
    {
        /* PQerrorMessage's result includes a trailing newline */
        fprintf(stderr, "%s", PQerrorMessage(conn));
        PQfinish(conn);
        return 1;
    }

    /* called once on any connection that should receive events.
     * Sends a PGEVT_REGISTER to myEventProc.
     */
    if (!PQregisterEventProc(conn, myEventProc, "mydata_proc", NULL))
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "Cannot register PGEventProc\n");
        PQfinish(conn);
        return 1;
    }

    /* conn instanceData is available */
    data = PQinstanceData(conn, myEventProc);

    /* Sends a PGEVT_RESULTCREATE to myEventProc */
    res = PQexec(conn, "SELECT 1 + 1");

    /* result instanceData is available */
    data = PQresultInstanceData(res, myEventProc);

    /* If PG_COPYRES_EVENTS is used, sends a PGEVT_RESULTCOPY to myEventProc */
    PGresult *res_copy;
    res_copy = PQcopyResult(res, PG_COPYRES_TUPLES | PG_COPYRES_EVENTS);

    /* result instanceData is available if PG_COPYRES_EVENTS was
     * used during the PQcopyResult call.
     */
    data = PQresultInstanceData(res_copy, myEventProc);

    /* Both clears send a PGEVT_RESULTDESTROY to myEventProc */
    PQclear(res);
    PQclear(res_copy);

    /* Sends a PGEVT_CONNDESTROY to myEventProc */
    PQfinish(conn);

    return 0;
}

static int
myEventProc(PGEventId evtId, void *evtInfo, void *passThrough)
{
    switch (evtId)
    {
        case PGEVT_REGISTER:
        {
            PGEventRegister *e = (PGEventRegister *)evtInfo;
            mydata *data = get_mydata(e->conn);

            /* associate app specific data with connection */
            PQsetInstanceData(e->conn, myEventProc, data);
            break;
        }

        case PGEVT_CONNRESET:
        {
            PGEventConnReset *e = (PGEventConnReset *)evtInfo;
            mydata *data = PQinstanceData(e->conn, myEventProc);

            if (data)
              memset(data, 0, sizeof(mydata));
            break;
        }

        case PGEVT_CONNDESTROY:
        {
            PGEventConnDestroy *e = (PGEventConnDestroy *)evtInfo;
            mydata *data = PQinstanceData(e->conn, myEventProc);

            /* free instance data because the conn is being destroyed */
            if (data)
              free_mydata(data);
            break;
        }

        case PGEVT_RESULTCREATE:
        {
            PGEventResultCreate *e = (PGEventResultCreate *)evtInfo;
            mydata *conn_data = PQinstanceData(e->conn, myEventProc);
            mydata *res_data = dup_mydata(conn_data);

            /* associate app specific data with result (copy it from conn) */
            PQresultSetInstanceData(e->result, myEventProc, res_data);
            break;
        }

        case PGEVT_RESULTCOPY:
        {
            PGEventResultCopy *e = (PGEventResultCopy *)evtInfo;
            mydata *src_data = PQresultInstanceData(e->src, myEventProc);
            mydata *dest_data = dup_mydata(src_data);

            /* associate app specific data with result (copy it from a result) */
            PQresultSetInstanceData(e->dest, myEventProc, dest_data);
            break;
        }

        case PGEVT_RESULTDESTROY:
        {
            PGEventResultDestroy *e = (PGEventResultDestroy *)evtInfo;
            mydata *data = PQresultInstanceData(e->result, myEventProc);

            /* free instance data because the result is being destroyed */
            if (data)
              free_mydata(data);
            break;
        }

        /* unknown event ID, just return true. */
        default:
            break;
    }

    return true; /* event processing succeeded */
}

/* Dummy implementations for helper functions (replace with actual logic) */
mydata *get_mydata(PGconn *conn) {
    mydata *data = malloc(sizeof(mydata));
    data->n = 1;
    data->str = strdup("initial data");
    return data;
}

mydata *dup_mydata(mydata *src) {
    if (!src) return NULL;
    mydata *data = malloc(sizeof(mydata));
    data->n = src->n;
    data->str = strdup(src->str);
    return data;
}

void free_mydata(mydata *data) {
    if (data) {
        free(data->str);
        free(data);
    }
}

```

                                       QUERY PLAN

   ->  Append  (cost=0.00..3.06 rows=3 width=14)
         ->  Seq Scan on gtest_child gtest_parent_1  (cost=0.00..1.01 rows=1 width=14)
               Filter: (f2 = 101)

### PostgreSQL: Control Constraint Exclusion for Query Planning

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/runtime-config-query

Demonstrates how to use PostgreSQL's constraint_exclusion parameter to optimize queries by leveraging table constraints. When enabled, the planner can skip scanning tables whose constraints contradict query conditions. The example shows a table inheritance setup where constraint exclusion prevents scanning irrelevant child tables.

```SQL
CREATE TABLE parent(key integer, ...);
CREATE TABLE child1000(check (key between 1000 and 1999)) INHERITS(parent);
CREATE TABLE child2000(check (key between 2000 and 2999)) INHERITS(parent);
...
SELECT * FROM parent WHERE key = 2400;
```

### Requesting LWLocks After Startup - C

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/xfunc-c

Obtain LWLocks after server startup by first allocating a tranche ID, then initializing each LWLock with the tranche ID. Finally, associate the tranche ID with a name. Ensure exclusive access during initialization to avoid race conditions.

```c
/* Allocate a new tranche ID */
int new_tranche_id = LWLockNewTrancheId();

/* Initialize an LWLock */
void LWLockInitialize(LWLock *lock, int tranche_id);

/* Register a name for the tranche */
void LWLockRegisterTranche(int tranche_id, const char *tranche_name);
```

### PostgreSQL START TRANSACTION Syntax

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-start-transaction

This snippet shows the basic syntax for the START TRANSACTION command in PostgreSQL. It outlines the optional transaction modes that can be specified when starting a new transaction block.

```sql
START TRANSACTION [ _transaction_mode_ [, ...] ]

where _transaction_mode_ is one of:

    ISOLATION LEVEL { SERIALIZABLE | REPEATABLE READ | READ COMMITTED | READ UNCOMMITTED }
    READ WRITE | READ ONLY
    [ NOT ] DEFERRABLE

```

### Get PostgreSQL Statement Statistics

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/pgstatstatements

Retrieves detailed statistics for executed SQL statements. Access to certain fields like SQL text and queryid is restricted to superusers or roles with `pg_read_all_stats` privileges for queries executed by other users. Statistics are available to other users if the view is installed in their database.

```APIDOC
## GET /websites/postgresql_current

### Description
Retrieves statistics for SQL statements executed on the PostgreSQL server.

### Method
GET

### Endpoint
/websites/postgresql_current

### Parameters
#### Query Parameters
- **fields** (string) - Optional - Comma-separated list of fields to retrieve. Available fields include:
    - `temp_blk_write_time`: Total time spent writing temporary file blocks (milliseconds).
    - `wal_records`: Total number of WAL records generated.
    - `wal_fpi`: Total number of WAL full page images generated.
    - `wal_bytes`: Total amount of WAL generated (bytes).
    - `wal_buffers_full`: Number of times WAL buffers became full.
    - `jit_functions`: Total number of functions JIT-compiled.
    - `jit_generation_time`: Total time spent on generating JIT code (milliseconds).
    - `jit_inlining_count`: Number of times functions have been inlined.
    - `jit_inlining_time`: Total time spent on inlining functions (milliseconds).
    - `jit_optimization_count`: Number of times the statement has been optimized.
    - `jit_optimization_time`: Total time spent on optimizing (milliseconds).
    - `jit_emission_count`: Number of times code has been emitted.
    - `jit_emission_time`: Total time spent on emitting code (milliseconds).
    - `jit_deform_count`: Number of tuple deform functions JIT-compiled.
    - `jit_deform_time`: Total time spent on JIT-compiling tuple deform functions (milliseconds).
    - `parallel_workers_to_launch`: Number of parallel workers planned to be launched.
    - `parallel_workers_launched`: Number of parallel workers actually launched.
    - `stats_since`: Time when statistics gathering started.
    - `minmax_stats_since`: Time when min/max statistics gathering started.

### Response
#### Success Response (200)
Returns a JSON object containing the requested statement statistics.

#### Response Example
```json
{
  "temp_blk_write_time": 10.5,
  "wal_records": 5000,
  "wal_fpi": 100,
  "wal_bytes": 204800,
  "wal_buffers_full": 5,
  "jit_functions": 10,
  "jit_generation_time": 25.3,
  "jit_inlining_count": 20,
  "jit_inlining_time": 15.1,
  "jit_optimization_count": 5,
  "jit_optimization_time": 10.0,
  "jit_emission_count": 15,
  "jit_emission_time": 5.5,
  "jit_deform_count": 2,
  "jit_deform_time": 2.0,
  "parallel_workers_to_launch": 4,
  "parallel_workers_launched": 4,
  "stats_since": "2023-10-27T10:00:00Z",
  "minmax_stats_since": "2023-10-27T09:55:00Z"
}
```
```

### Example Injection Point Callback (C)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/xfunc-c

An example of a callback function for an injection point. This callback logs a notice message and reports wait events using PostgreSQL's logging and statistics reporting functions.

```c
static void
custom_injection_callback(const char *name,
                          const void *private_data,
                          void *arg)
{
    uint32 wait_event_info = WaitEventInjectionPointNew(name);

    pgstat_report_wait_start(wait_event_info);
    elog(NOTICE, "%s: executed custom callback", name);
    pgstat_report_wait_end();
}
```

### Create Indexes on Partitioned Tables with CONCURRENTLY

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ddl-partitioning

Demonstrates how to create indexes on partitioned tables by first creating them on individual partitions using `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` and then attaching them to a parent index on the partitioned table. This workaround avoids long lock times associated with directly creating indexes on partitioned tables.

```sql
CREATE INDEX measurement_usls_idx ON ONLY measurement (unitsales);

CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY measurement_usls_200602_idx
    ON measurement_y2006m02 (unitsales);
ALTER INDEX measurement_usls_idx
    ATTACH PARTITION measurement_usls_200602_idx;
...
```

### Get Data Type OID for a Column (C)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-exec

The PQftype function returns the internal Object ID (OID) of the data type for a given column number. Column numbers start at 0. This OID can be used to query system tables like pg_type for more type information.

```c
Oid PQftype(const PGresult *res,
            int column_number);
```

### Avoid Runtime Sentence Construction in C

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/nls-programmer

This example shows an incorrect way to construct sentences at runtime using printf, which can lead to translation issues due to differing word orders and fragmenting translatable units. It's better to keep messages as coherent wholes and insert variables at runtime.

```c
printf("Files were %s.\n", flag ? "copied" : "removed");

```

### PostgreSQL Event Trigger for Table Rewrite

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-event-triggers

This example demonstrates how to create an event trigger in PostgreSQL that executes a function when a table rewrite event occurs. The function uses pg_event_trigger_table_rewrite_oid() to get the table OID and pg_event_trigger_table_rewrite_reason() to determine the rewrite reason.

```sql
CREATE FUNCTION test_event_trigger_table_rewrite_oid()
 RETURNS event_trigger
 LANGUAGE plpgsql AS
$$
BEGIN
  RAISE NOTICE 'rewriting table % for reason %',
                pg_event_trigger_table_rewrite_oid()::regclass,
                pg_event_trigger_table_rewrite_reason();
END;
$$
;

CREATE EVENT TRIGGER test_table_rewrite_oid
                  ON table_rewrite
   EXECUTE FUNCTION test_event_trigger_table_rewrite_oid();

```

### PostgreSQL: Table Definitions for Row Security Example

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ddl-rowsecurity

These SQL statements define the 'groups', 'users', and 'information' tables, along with their relationships and initial data. This setup is used to illustrate advanced row security policies that involve checking data in other tables.

```sql
-- definition of privilege groups
CREATE TABLE groups (
  group_id int PRIMARY KEY,
  group_name text NOT NULL
);

INSERT INTO groups VALUES
  (1, 'low'),
  (2, 'medium'),
  (5, 'high');

GRANT ALL ON groups TO alice;  -- alice is the administrator
GRANT SELECT ON groups TO public;

-- definition of users' privilege levels
CREATE TABLE users (
  user_name text PRIMARY KEY,
  group_id int NOT NULL REFERENCES groups
);

INSERT INTO users VALUES
  ('alice', 5),
  ('bob', 2),
  ('mallory', 2);

GRANT ALL ON users TO alice;
GRANT SELECT ON users TO public;

-- table holding the information to be protected
CREATE TABLE information (
  info text,
  group_id int NOT NULL REFERENCES groups
);

INSERT INTO information VALUES
  ('barely secret', 1),
  ('slightly secret', 2),
  ('very secret', 5);

ALTER TABLE information ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
```

### EXPLAIN Query Plan Analysis (PostgreSQL)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/row-estimation-examples

Provides an example of an EXPLAIN output for a query involving a join. This helps in understanding how the planner breaks down the query execution, including nested loop joins and bitmap scans, and their associated costs and row estimates.

```sql
EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM tenk1 t1, tenk2 t2
WHERE t1.unique1 < 50 AND t1.unique2 = t2.unique2;

                                      QUERY PLAN
-------------------------------------------------------------------
 Nested Loop  (cost=4.64..456.23 rows=50 width=488)
   ->  Bitmap Heap Scan on tenk1 t1  (cost=4.64..142.17 rows=50 width=244)
         Recheck Cond: (unique1 < 50)
         ->  Bitmap Index Scan on tenk1_unique1  (cost=0.00..4.63 rows=50 width=0)
               Index Cond: (unique1 < 50)
   ->  Index Scan using tenk2_unique2 on tenk2 t2  (cost=0.00..6.27 rows=1 width=244)
         Index Cond: (unique2 = t1.unique2)
```

### Get Base Type of a Domain - PostgreSQL

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-info

The `pg_basetype` function returns the OID of the base type for a given domain OID. If the input is not a domain, it returns the input OID. It recursively finds the base type in case of domain dependencies. Example: `pg_basetype('mytext'::regtype)` returns the OID for `text`.

```SQL
SELECT pg_basetype('my_domain_type'::regtype);
```

### PostgreSQL array_position Function

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-array

Returns the subscript of the first occurrence of a given element in a one-dimensional array, or NULL if not found. The search can start from a specified subscript. Example: array_position(ARRAY['sun', 'mon', 'tue', 'wed', 'thu', 'fri', 'sat'], 'mon') returns 2.

```sql
SELECT array_position(ARRAY['sun', 'mon', 'tue', 'wed', 'thu', 'fri', 'sat'], 'mon');

-- Result: |       bar|

-- Result: |       foo|
```

### Register PostgreSQL Background Worker (C)

```c
// Register background worker at PostgreSQL start
RegisterBackgroundWorker(&my_worker);

// Register dynamic background worker after PostgreSQL is running
BackgroundWorkerHandle *handle;
RegisterDynamicBackgroundWorker(&my_worker, &handle);

### ECPG Example: Preparing, Describing, and Fetching with SQLDA (C)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ecpg-informix-compat

Provides a complete example using ECPG (Embedded C PostgreSQL) to prepare a SQL statement, describe its results into an SQLDA structure, and then fetch data row by row using the same SQLDA structure. It also shows memory management for the SQLDA.

```c
EXEC SQL INCLUDE sqlda.h;

    sqlda_t        *sqlda; /* This doesn't need to be under embedded DECLARE SECTION */

    EXEC SQL BEGIN DECLARE SECTION;
    char *prep_stmt = "select * from table1";
    int i;
    EXEC SQL END DECLARE SECTION;

    ...

    EXEC SQL PREPARE mystmt FROM :prep_stmt;

    EXEC SQL DESCRIBE mystmt INTO sqlda;

    printf("# of fields: %d\n", sqlda->sqld);
    for (i = 0; i < sqlda->sqld; i++)
      printf("field %d: \"%s\"\n", sqlda->sqlvar[i]->sqlname);

    EXEC SQL DECLARE mycursor CURSOR FOR mystmt;
    EXEC SQL OPEN mycursor;
    EXEC SQL WHENEVER NOT FOUND GOTO out;

    while (1)
    {
      EXEC SQL FETCH mycursor USING sqlda;
    }

    EXEC SQL CLOSE mycursor;

    free(sqlda); /* The main structure is all to be free(),
                  * sqlda and sqlda->sqlvar is in one allocated area */

out:

```

### PostgreSQL instr Function with Starting Position

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/plpgsql-porting

This PL/pgSQL function extends the 'instr' functionality by allowing a specified starting position for the search. It supports both positive (forward) and negative (backward) starting indices. If the starting index is positive, the search begins from that character. If negative, it starts from that many characters from the end of the string.

```plpgsql
CREATE FUNCTION instr(string varchar, string_to_search_for varchar,
                      beg_index integer)
RETURNS integer AS $$
DECLARE
    pos integer NOT NULL DEFAULT 0;
    temp_str varchar;
    beg integer;
    length integer;
    ss_length integer;
BEGIN
    IF beg_index > 0 THEN
        temp_str := substring(string FROM beg_index);
        pos := position(string_to_search_for IN temp_str);

        IF pos = 0 THEN
            RETURN 0;
        ELSE
            RETURN pos + beg_index - 1;
        END IF;
    ELSIF beg_index < 0 THEN
        ss_length := char_length(string_to_search_for);
        length := char_length(string);
        beg := length + 1 + beg_index;

        WHILE beg > 0 LOOP
            temp_str := substring(string FROM beg FOR ss_length);
            IF string_to_search_for = temp_str THEN
                RETURN beg;
            END IF;

            beg := beg - 1;
        END LOOP;

        RETURN 0;
    ELSE
        RETURN 0;
    END IF;
END;
$$ LANGUAGE plpgsql STRICT IMMUTABLE;
```

### PostgreSQL SPI Function Execution Examples

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/spi-examples

A series of SQL commands demonstrating the usage and behavior of the 'execq' PostgreSQL function. These examples showcase creating tables, inserting data, selecting data, and using the function's row count limitation, illustrating SPI's capabilities and data visibility rules.

```sql
=> SELECT execq('CREATE TABLE a (x integer)', 0);
 execq
-------
     0
(1 row)

=> INSERT INTO a VALUES (execq('INSERT INTO a VALUES (0)', 0));
INSERT 0 1
=> SELECT execq('SELECT * FROM a', 0);
INFO:  EXECQ:  0    _-- inserted by execq_
 execq
-------
     2
(1 row)

=> SELECT execq('INSERT INTO a SELECT x + 2 FROM a RETURNING *', 1);
INFO:  EXECQ:  2    _-- 0 + 2, then execution was stopped by count_
 execq
-------
     1
(1 row)

=> SELECT execq('SELECT * FROM a', 10);
INFO:  EXECQ:  0
INFO:  EXECQ:  1
INFO:  EXECQ:  2

 execq
-------
     3              _-- 10 is the max value only, 3 is the real number of rows_
(1 row)

=> SELECT execq('INSERT INTO a SELECT x + 10 FROM a', 1);
 execq
-------
     3              _-- all rows processed; count does not stop it, because nothing is returned_
(1 row)

=> SELECT * FROM a;
 x
----
  0
  1
  2
 10
 11
 12
(6 rows)

=> DELETE FROM a;
DELETE 6
=> INSERT INTO a VALUES (execq('SELECT * FROM a', 0) + 1);
INSERT 0 1
=> SELECT * FROM a;
 x
---
 1
(1 row)

=> INSERT INTO a VALUES (execq('SELECT * FROM a', 0) + 1);
INFO:  EXECQ:  1
INSERT 0 1
=> SELECT * FROM a;
 x
---
 1
 2                  _-- 1 (there was one row in a) + 1_
(2 rows)

_-- This demonstrates the data changes visibility rule._
_-- execq is called twice and sees different numbers of rows each time:_

=> INSERT INTO a SELECT execq('SELECT * FROM a', 0) * x FROM a;
INFO:  EXECQ:  1    _-- results from first execq_
INFO:  EXECQ:  2
INFO:  EXECQ:  1    _-- results from second execq_
INFO:  EXECQ:  2
INFO:  EXECQ:  2
INSERT 0 2
=> SELECT * FROM a;
 x
---
 1
 2
 2                  _-- 2 rows * 1 (x in first row)_
 6                  _-- 3 rows (2 + 1 just inserted) * 2 (x in second row)_
(4 rows)


```

### Create PostgreSQL Subscription and Table

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/logical-replication-row-filter

This snippet shows the setup on a subscriber node. It includes creating a table `t1` with the same definition as on the publisher and then establishing a subscription `s1` to replicate data from publication `p1` hosted on a remote server.

```sql
/* sub # */ CREATE TABLE t1(a int, b int, c text, PRIMARY KEY(a,c));
/* sub # */ CREATE SUBSCRIPTION s1
/* sub - */ CONNECTION 'host=localhost dbname=test_pub application_name=s1'
/* sub - */ PUBLICATION p1;
```

### Initialize SQLDA and Connect to PostgreSQL Database

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ecpg-descriptors

This snippet initializes SQLDA structures for input and output, includes necessary headers, defines the SQL query, and establishes a connection to the PostgreSQL database. It also sets the search path and prepares the SQL statement.

```c
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <unistd.h>

EXEC SQL include sqlda.h;

sqlda_t *sqlda1; /* descriptor for output */
sqlda_t *sqlda2; /* descriptor for input */

EXEC SQL WHENEVER NOT FOUND DO BREAK;
EXEC SQL WHENEVER SQLERROR STOP;

int
main(void)
{
    EXEC SQL BEGIN DECLARE SECTION;
    char query[1024] = "SELECT d.oid,* FROM pg_database d, pg_stat_database s WHERE d.oid=s.datid AND ( d.datname=? OR d.oid=? )";

    int intval;
    unsigned long long int longlongval;
    EXEC SQL END DECLARE SECTION;

    EXEC SQL CONNECT TO uptimedb AS con1 USER uptime;

```

### PL/Tcl Transaction Control Example

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/pltcl-transactions

This PL/Tcl procedure demonstrates how to control transactions by inserting data and conditionally committing or rolling back based on a loop's iteration. It uses the `commit` and `rollback` commands specific to PL/Tcl. A new transaction is automatically started after each commit or rollback.

```sql
CREATE PROCEDURE transaction_test1()
LANGUAGE pltcl
AS $$
for {set i 0} {$i < 10} {incr i} {
    spi_exec "INSERT INTO test1 (a) VALUES ($i)"
    if {$i % 2 == 0} {
        commit
    } else {
        rollback
    }
}
$$;

CALL transaction_test1();

```

--------------------------------

### CREATE TRIGGER - Basic Example

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createtrigger

This example demonstrates how to create a simple row-level trigger that executes a function before an update operation on the 'accounts' table.

```APIDOC
## CREATE TRIGGER accounts - Basic Example

### Description
Executes the `check_account_update` function before each row update on the `accounts` table.

### Method
CREATE TRIGGER

### Endpoint
N/A (DDL Statement)

### Parameters
#### Trigger Event
- **BEFORE UPDATE ON accounts**: Specifies that the trigger should fire before an `UPDATE` operation on the `accounts` table.
- **FOR EACH ROW**: Indicates that the trigger should execute once for each row affected by the `UPDATE` statement.

### Action
- **EXECUTE FUNCTION check_account_update()**: Calls the specified function when the trigger event occurs.

### Request Example
```sql
CREATE TRIGGER check_update
    BEFORE UPDATE ON accounts
    FOR EACH ROW
    EXECUTE FUNCTION check_account_update();
```

### Response
N/A (DDL Statement)
```

--------------------------------

### START TRANSACTION Command

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-start-transaction

This section details the START TRANSACTION command used to initiate a new transaction block in PostgreSQL. It covers the command's syntax, different transaction modes, and its behavior compared to the BEGIN command.

```APIDOC
## START TRANSACTION

### Description
Starts a new transaction block. If transaction modes are specified, the new transaction inherits those characteristics, similar to executing `SET TRANSACTION`.

### Method
SQL Command

### Endpoint
N/A (SQL Command)

### Parameters
#### Transaction Modes
- **ISOLATION LEVEL** (SERIALIZABLE | REPEATABLE READ | READ COMMITTED | READ UNCOMMITTED) - Specifies the transaction isolation level.
- **READ WRITE | READ ONLY** - Determines if the transaction can write or only read data.
- **[NOT] DEFERRABLE** - Controls whether the transaction's constraints are deferred.

*Note: Refer to `SET TRANSACTION` for detailed explanations of these parameters.*

### Request Example
```sql
START TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ READ WRITE;
```

### Response
#### Success Response
Transactions are managed implicitly by subsequent SQL commands or explicit `COMMIT`/`ROLLBACK`.

#### Response Example
(No direct response; transaction begins and is managed by subsequent commands.)

### Compatibility
- The `DEFERRABLE` mode is a PostgreSQL extension.
- PostgreSQL historically allows omitting commas between transaction modes.
- Implicit transaction blocks are standard, but PostgreSQL's autocommit behavior is noted.
```

### Get psql Help Information (Bash)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/app-psql

The \? meta-command provides help within psql. It can display information on 'commands', 'options', or 'variables'. If no topic is specified, it defaults to providing help on psql's backslash commands.

```Bash
# Get general help on psql commands
psql> \?

# Get help on command-line options
psql> \? options

# Get help on configuration variables
psql> \? variables
```

### PostgreSQL SRF Pseudo-code Example

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/xfunc-c

This pseudo-code outlines the general structure of a Set Returning Function (SRF) in PostgreSQL. It demonstrates the use of SRF_IS_FIRSTCALL, SRF_FIRSTCALL_INIT, SRF_PERCALL_SETUP, SRF_RETURN_NEXT, and SRF_RETURN_DONE macros for managing function state across calls. It also highlights memory context management for arguments.

```c
Datum
my_set_returning_function(PG_FUNCTION_ARGS)
{
    FuncCallContext  *funcctx;
    Datum             result;
    /* further declarations as needed */

    if (SRF_IS_FIRSTCALL())
    {
        MemoryContext oldcontext;

        funcctx = SRF_FIRSTCALL_INIT();
        oldcontext = MemoryContextSwitchTo(funcctx->multi_call_memory_ctx);
        /* One-time setup code appears here: */
        /* _user code_ */
        /* _if returning composite_ */
            /* _build TupleDesc, and perhaps AttInMetadata_ */
        /* _endif returning composite_ */
        /* _user code_ */
        MemoryContextSwitchTo(oldcontext);
    }

    /* Each-time setup code appears here: */
    /* _user code_ */
    funcctx = SRF_PERCALL_SETUP();
    /* _user code_ */

    /* this is just one way we might test whether we are done: */
    if (funcctx->call_cntr < funcctx->max_calls)
    {
        /* Here we want to return another item: */
        /* _user code_ */
        /* _obtain result Datum_ */
        SRF_RETURN_NEXT(funcctx, result);
    }
    else
    {
        /* Here we are done returning items, so just report that fact. */
        /* (Resist the temptation to put cleanup code here.) */
        SRF_RETURN_DONE(funcctx);
    }
}

```

### PostgreSQL Event Trigger Example

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createeventtrigger

An example demonstrating how to create an event trigger in PostgreSQL. This specific example forbids the execution of any DDL command by raising an exception when the 'ddl_command_start' event occurs.

```plpgsql
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION abort_any_command()
  RETURNS event_trigger
 LANGUAGE plpgsql
  AS $$
BEGIN
  RAISE EXCEPTION 'command % is disabled', tg_tag;
END;
$$;

CREATE EVENT TRIGGER abort_ddl ON ddl_command_start
   EXECUTE FUNCTION abort_any_command();
```
