### Create and Analyze B-tree Index in PostgreSQL

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/bloom

Demonstrates creating a composite B-tree index on multiple columns and analyzing its size. It then shows that even with this index, a selective query might still result in a sequential scan due to the query pattern not matching the leading index columns effectively.

```sql
CREATE INDEX btreeidx ON tbloom (i1, i2, i3, i4, i5, i6);
SELECT pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size('btreeidx'));
EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM tbloom WHERE i2 = 898732 AND i5 = 123451;
```

### Rewriting View Operations with PostgreSQL Rules

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/rules-update

This example shows how to use PostgreSQL rules to rewrite INSERT, UPDATE, and DELETE operations on a view ('shoelace') to perform corresponding actions on an underlying table ('shoelace_data'). The `DO INSTEAD` clause specifies the alternative operations.

```sql
CREATE RULE shoelace_ins AS ON INSERT TO shoelace
    DO INSTEAD
    INSERT INTO shoelace_data VALUES (
           NEW.sl_name,
           NEW.sl_avail,
           NEW.sl_color,
           NEW.sl_len,
           NEW.sl_unit
    );

CREATE RULE shoelace_upd AS ON UPDATE TO shoelace
    DO INSTEAD
    UPDATE shoelace_data
       SET sl_name = NEW.sl_name,
           sl_avail = NEW.sl_avail,
           sl_color = NEW.sl_color,
           sl_len = NEW.sl_len,
           sl_unit = NEW.sl_unit
     WHERE sl_name = OLD.sl_name;

CREATE RULE shoelace_del AS ON DELETE TO shoelace
    DO INSTEAD
    DELETE FROM shoelace_data
     WHERE sl_name = OLD.sl_name;
```

### PostgreSQL Row-Valued Expression NULL Checks

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-comparison

```sql
SELECT ROW(1,2.5,'this is a test') = ROW(1, 3, 'not the same');

SELECT ROW(table.*) IS NULL FROM table;  -- detect all-null rows

SELECT ROW(table.*) IS NOT NULL FROM table;  -- detect all-non-null rows

SELECT NOT(ROW(table.*) IS NOT NULL) FROM TABLE; -- detect at least one null in rows
```

## COPY FROM Behavior with Triggers and Constraints

`COPY FROM` will invoke any triggers and check constraints on the destination table. However, it will not invoke rules.

For identity columns, the `COPY FROM` command will always write the column values provided in the input data, like the `INSERT` option `OVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUE`.

### CREATE TRIGGER - INSTEAD OF INSERT on View

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createtrigger

This example demonstrates creating an INSTEAD OF trigger for an INSERT operation on a view, which executes a function to handle the insertion into underlying tables.

```APIDOC
## CREATE TRIGGER my_view - INSTEAD OF INSERT

### Description
Creates an `INSTEAD OF INSERT` trigger on `my_view`. This trigger executes a function for each row intended for insertion into the view, allowing custom logic to insert data into the underlying tables.

### Method
CREATE TRIGGER

### Endpoint
N/A (DDL Statement)

### Parameters
#### Trigger Event
- **INSTEAD OF INSERT ON my_view**: Specifies that the trigger should fire instead of the default `INSERT` action on `my_view`.
- **FOR EACH ROW**: Indicates that the trigger should execute once for each row that would have been inserted.

### Action
- **EXECUTE FUNCTION view_insert_row()**: Calls the specified function to handle the insertion logic.

### Request Example
```sql
CREATE TRIGGER view_insert
    INSTEAD OF INSERT ON my_view
    FOR EACH ROW
    EXECUTE FUNCTION view_insert_row();
```

### Response
N/A (DDL Statement)
```

### PostgreSQL: SUBSTRING with Greedy vs. Non-Greedy Quantifiers

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/functions-matching

Demonstrates the difference between greedy and non-greedy quantifiers in PostgreSQL's SUBSTRING function. The first example uses a greedy quantifier 'Y*' to match the longest possible substring, while the second uses a non-greedy 'Y*?' to match the shortest. This highlights how greediness affects the captured group.

```sql
SELECT SUBSTRING('XY1234Z', 'Y*([0-9]{1,3})');
-- Result: 123

SELECT SUBSTRING('XY1234Z', 'Y*?([0-9]{1,3})');
-- Result: 1
```

### PostgreSQL C Function: Create Point

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/xfunc-c

An example of a version 1 PostgreSQL function that creates a `Point` struct. It demonstrates accessing `Point` arguments by reference using `PG_GETARG_POINT_P`, allocating memory with `palloc`, and returning a pointer to the new `Point` using `PG_RETURN_POINT_P`.

```c
PG_FUNCTION_INFO_V1(makepoint);

Datum
makepoint(PG_FUNCTION_ARGS)
{
    /* Here, the pass-by-reference nature of Point is not hidden. */
    Point     *pointx = PG_GETARG_POINT_P(0);
    Point     *pointy = PG_GETARG_POINT_P(1);
    Point     *new_point = (Point *) palloc(sizeof(Point));

    new_point->x = pointx->x;
    new_point->y = pointy->y;

    PG_RETURN_POINT_P(new_point);
}
```

### Indexing with Daitch-Mokotoff Soundex using GIN

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/fuzzystrmatch

This section illustrates how to create a GIN index on the Daitch-Mokotoff soundex codes of a text column for efficient searching. It includes table creation, index definition, data insertion, and example queries using the '&&' operator for array overlap matching.

```SQL
CREATE TABLE s (nm text);
CREATE INDEX ix_s_dm ON s USING gin (daitch_mokotoff(nm)) WITH (fastupdate = off);

INSERT INTO s (nm) VALUES
  ('Schwartzenegger'),
  ('John'),
  ('James'),
  ('Steinman'),
  ('Steinmetz');

SELECT * FROM s WHERE daitch_mokotoff(nm) && daitch_mokotoff('Swartzenegger');
SELECT * FROM s WHERE daitch_mokotoff(nm) && daitch_mokotoff('Jane');
SELECT * FROM s WHERE daitch_mokotoff(nm) && daitch_mokotoff('Jens');
```

--------------------------------

### PostgreSQL crosstab3 Example

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/tablefunc

Demonstrates using the `crosstab3` function with specific SQL query to pivot data. This function is part of the `tablefunc` module and simplifies pivoting by predefining output column types.

```sql
SELECT *
FROM crosstab3(
  'select rowid, attribute, value
   from ct
   where attribute = ''att2'' or attribute = ''att3''
   order by 1,2');

```

### PostgreSQL: Get Command Status with GET DIAGNOSTICS

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/plpgsql-statements

Retrieves system status indicators after executing an SQL command. It allows access to values like the number of rows processed (`ROW_COUNT`), the current call stack (`PG_CONTEXT`), and the OID of the current function (`PG_ROUTINE_OID`). The `CURRENT` keyword is optional, and `:=` can be used as an alternative to `=`. The specified variable must be of the correct data type to receive the status item.

```SQL
GET [ CURRENT ] DIAGNOSTICS _variable_ { = | := } _item_ [ , ... ];

GET DIAGNOSTICS integer_var = ROW_COUNT;
```

### Export PostgreSQL Large Object to File using libpq (C)

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/lo-examplesect

This function exports a PostgreSQL large object to a local file. It opens the specified large object for reading and creates/opens a local file for writing. Data is read from the large object in chunks and written to the local file. Both the large object and the local file are closed upon completion. Error handling for file and large object operations is included.

```c
/*
 * exportFile - 
 *    export large object "lobjOid" to file "out_filename"
 *
 */
static void
exportFile(PGconn *conn, Oid lobjId, char *filename)
{
    int         lobj_fd;
    char        buf[BUFSIZE];
    int         nbytes, 
                tmp;
    int         fd;

    /*
     * open the large object
     */
    lobj_fd = lo_open(conn, lobjId, INV_READ);
    if (lobj_fd < 0)
        fprintf(stderr, "cannot open large object %u", lobjId);

    /*
     * open the file to be written to
     */
    fd = open(filename, O_CREAT | O_WRONLY | O_TRUNC, 0666);
    if (fd < 0)
    {
        fprintf(stderr, "cannot open unix file\"%s\"",
                filename);
    }

    /*
     * read in from the inversion file and write to the Unix file
     */
    while ((nbytes = lo_read(conn, lobj_fd, buf, BUFSIZE)) > 0)
    {
        tmp = write(fd, buf, nbytes);
        if (tmp < nbytes)
        {
            fprintf(stderr, "error while writing \"%s\"",
                    filename);
        }
    }

    lo_close(conn, lobj_fd);
    close(fd);
}
```

### PQconnectdbParams

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/libpq-connect

Establishes a new database connection using keywords and values arrays for parameters. This is the preferred method for new applications due to its extensibility.

```APIDOC
## PQconnectdbParams

### Description
Makes a new connection to the database server using parameters provided in two NULL-terminated arrays: `keywords` and `values`. Allows for extending connection parameters without changing the function signature.

### Method
`PGconn *PQconnectdbParams(const char * const *keywords, const char * const *values, int expand_dbname);`

### Parameters
*   **keywords** (array of strings) - An array of parameter keywords. Processing stops at the first NULL entry.
*   **values** (array of strings) - An array of values corresponding to the `keywords`. NULL or empty strings are ignored.
*   **expand_dbname** (int) - If non-zero, the `dbname` keyword's value is checked to see if it's a connection string and expanded accordingly.

### Request Example
```json
{
  "keywords": ["host", "port", "dbname"],
  "values": ["localhost", "5432", "mydatabase"],
  "expand_dbname": 1
}
```

### Response
#### Success Response (PGconn)
*   **PGconn** (*PGconn) - A pointer to a connection structure if successful, otherwise NULL.
```

### Soundex and Difference Functions

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/fuzzystrmatch

Learn how to use the `soundex` function to generate Soundex codes and the `difference` function to compare the similarity of two strings based on their Soundex codes.

```APIDOC
## Soundex and Difference Functions

### Soundex

The Soundex system is a phonetic algorithm for indexing names by sound, as pronounced in English. The goal is for homophones to be encoded to the same representation so that they can be matched despite minor differences in spelling. The `soundex` function converts a string into its Soundex code.

### Difference

The `difference` function calculates the Soundex codes for two input strings and returns the number of matching characters in the codes. The result ranges from 0 (no match) to 4 (exact match). A higher number indicates greater similarity.

### Usage Examples

#### Generating Soundex Codes
```sql
SELECT soundex('hello world!');
```

#### Comparing String Similarity using Difference
```sql
SELECT soundex('Anne'), soundex('Ann'), difference('Anne', 'Ann');
SELECT soundex('Anne'), soundex('Andrew'), difference('Anne', 'Andrew');
SELECT soundex('Anne'), soundex('Margaret'), difference('Anne', 'Margaret');
```

#### Using Soundex in Queries
```sql
CREATE TABLE s (nm text);
INSERT INTO s VALUES ('john'), ('joan'), ('wobbly'), ('jack');

-- Select names with the same Soundex code as 'john'
SELECT * FROM s WHERE soundex(nm) = soundex('john');

-- Select names that have a difference score greater than 2 when compared to 'john'
SELECT * FROM s WHERE difference(s.nm, 'john') > 2;
```
```

### lquery Modifier Examples

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/ltree

```plaintext
@ 		_Match case-insensitively, for example a@ matches A_
* 		_Match any label with this prefix, for example foo* matches foobar_
% 		_Match initial underscore-separated words_

```

```

### Insert or Do Nothing on Conflict in PostgreSQL

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-insert

Shows how to insert a new distributor or do nothing if a conflict occurs on the 'did' column. This example uses a partial unique index predicate to only apply the 'DO NOTHING' behavior for active distributors.

```sql
-- This statement could infer a partial unique index on "did"
-- with a predicate of "WHERE is_active", but it could also
-- just use a regular unique constraint on "did"
INSERT INTO distributors (did, dname) VALUES (10, 'Conrad International')
    ON CONFLICT (did) WHERE is_active DO NOTHING;

```

### PL/pgSQL IF-THEN-ELSE Statement Example

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/plpgsql-control-structures

Shows the IF-THEN-ELSE construct in PL/pgSQL, allowing for an alternative block of statements to be executed when the condition is false. This is suitable for binary decisions.

```plpgsql
IF parentid IS NULL OR parentid = ''
THEN
    RETURN fullname;
ELSE
    RETURN hp_true_filename(parentid) || '/' || fullname;
END IF;
```

```plpgsql
IF v_count > 0 THEN
    INSERT INTO users_count (count) VALUES (v_count);
    RETURN 't';
ELSE
    RETURN 'f';
END IF;
```

### SELinux: PostgreSQL `sepgsql_setcon` Function

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sepgsql

The `sepgsql_setcon()` function allows a PostgreSQL client session to transition to a new SELinux security context, provided the SELinux policy permits it. It accepts a text string representing the target security context. Passing `NULL` as an argument requests a transition back to the client's original domain. This function returns a boolean indicating success or failure.

```sql
SELECT sepgsql_setcon('target_security_context');
SELECT sepgsql_setcon(NULL);
```

### Define PL/pgSQL Function to Increment Integer

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/sql-createfunction

Shows how to define a function in PL/pgSQL that increments an integer input by one. This example uses `CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION` and specifies the language as `plpgsql`.

```PL/pgSQL
CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION increment(i integer) RETURNS integer AS $$
        BEGIN
                RETURN i + 1;
        END;
$$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;
```

### PostgreSQL: Configure Foreign Data Wrapper for dblink

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/contrib-dblink-connect

Shows the SQL commands to set up a foreign data wrapper (FDW) using dblink. This involves creating a server definition, a user mapping with credentials, and granting necessary privileges. This setup is essential for querying remote tables as if they were local.

```sql
CREATE SERVER fdtest FOREIGN DATA WRAPPER dblink_fdw OPTIONS (hostaddr '127.0.0.1', dbname 'contrib_regression');
CREATE USER regress_dblink_user WITH PASSWORD 'secret';
CREATE USER MAPPING FOR regress_dblink_user SERVER fdtest OPTIONS (user 'regress_dblink_user', password 'secret');
GRANT USAGE ON FOREIGN SERVER fdtest TO regress_dblink_user;
GRANT SELECT ON TABLE foo TO regress_dblink_user;
```

### PostgreSQL ts_headline Example 1: Basic Usage

Source: https://www.postgresql.org/docs/current/textsearch-controls

Demonstrates the basic usage of the `ts_headline` function in PostgreSQL to highlight search query terms within a text. It takes the language, the text content, and a `to_tsquery` object as input. The output shows the highlighted terms using default XML tags.

```sql
SELECT ts_headline('english',
  'The most common type of search
is to find all documents containing given query terms
and return them in order of their similarity to the
query.',
  to_tsquery('english', 'query & similarity'));
```

This command vacuums a specific table named 'onek', analyzes it for the query optimizer, and provides a detailed report of the vacuuming activity. It requires the 'MAINTAIN' privilege on the table or database ownership.
